Text:"Socratic Skill Learning: Interleaving Teaching and Practice for Transferable Agent Skills Letian Zhu1, Dianyi Wang1 Yucheng Fu1 Jiakai Wang2 Zhiqi Bai2 Wenbo Su2 Jiaheng Liu1, Xinping Lei Xinyu Che1 Jiahao Wang1 Yifan Yao1, Han Li1, 1 Nanjing University 2 Alibaba Group Equal contribution. Corresponding author. Agent Skills improve LLM agents by making task knowledge reusable across sessions. Learning transferable Skills requires evidence about procedure and its conditions of use, which successful task attempt can leave untested. We introduce Socratic Skill Learning (SSL), which interleaves expert teaching with Student practice. An Educator guides investigation while the Student remains the sole environment operator and Skill author, able to question, test, and adapt advice. To test reuse beyond the learning task, we construct SKILLTRANSFERBENCH, with 35 task families and 408 executable cases, pairing public variation specifications with held-out evaluation in fresh solver sessions. On the main 22-family, 155-case cohort, Fixed-Window SSL reaches 0.801 utility, versus 0.723 without Skill, 0.761 for adapted Trace2Skill (trajectory distillation), and 0.751 for adapted SKILL-KD (contrastive distillation). Across three model pairs on shared families, interleaved guidance outperforms expert authoring and post-hoc teaching. Process analysis links guidance to Student writing and package revision; case study shows the Student testing and refining proposed procedure for later reuse. As demand for new lessons falls, the cost of fixed-window review grows. Issue-Guided SSL tracks unresolved questions across Skill revisions to select and focus review, reaching 0.805 utility with 61.7% fewer Educator tokens and 16.9% fewer total acquisition tokens than Fixed-Window SSL on the main cohort. Correspondence: Jiaheng Liu at liujiaheng@nju.edu.cn Date: October 12, 2026 Code: https://github.com/NJU-LINK/Socratic-Skill-Learning Agent Skills extend LLM agents through reusable instructions "

4 0 2 , . t a i ( i e e i o f t g i m d a u r p h g a , r w . ) 6 0 2 , . t a Z ; 5 2 0 , T u o n m g f u l u e i e o e o i i y l r d b i e d k o e e ; 6 2 2 , . t n M ; 2 2 0 2 , . e e ( d w m a t t e ff s c s t o u m o e v a e c s s r c a y p a o ) ( , e M ; 5 0 2 , . t a ; 4 2 0 2 , . e n a t - d n n d R A ( n l e , m y e t s A . ) 6 2 0 2 , . e u i o t i e e f g n m n i d r 1 Figure 1. One physical world versus diverse virtual worlds. Left: Agents learn from varied physical scenes sharing common dynamics, where they can scale and generalize with broader experience (Wang et al., 2026a). Right: Agents play diverse games with varying dynamics. We want to investigate the question that whether continued pre-training scales and generalizes in virtual worlds. 2025; Tan et al., 2025). However, virtual embodied continued pre-training remains largely recipe: existing studies change the data volume, diversity and models together, leaving the effect of each factor unresolved. Most importantly, it remains unclear whether heterogeneous experiences help to build embodied agents that can generalize across different worlds, or if it just causes interference that ruins what they learned in any individual worlds. We address this question with controlled grid study spanning four model sizes from 0.8B to 27B, six data budgets from 50 to 1,600 hours, and five data mixture settings from Minecraft-only to broad multi-game corpora. The resulting 120 configurations are evaluated through three-level validation loss and benchmark ladders from in-corpus worlds, such as Minecraft (Zheng et al., 2025) and Doom (Wydmuch et al., 2019), to unseen near and distant worlds, such as OmniGameArena (Lin et al., 2026), and GameWorld (Ouyang et al., 2026). Our study reveals how data volume, mixture diversity, and model capacity affect embodied agents in these three levels of worlds, and whether heterogeneous experience becomes complementary as it scales. Our contributions are mainly three-fold: controlled study of scaling CPT in virtual worlds. We quantify how data volume, mixture diversity, and model size affect CPT of generalist embodied agents in virtual worlds. More relevant data improves performance on in-corpus worlds where model performance multiplies in Minecraft. Diversity improves transfer to related worlds but provides limited gains in distant worlds. Larger models improve data efficiency and achieve larger early gains, while generalizing worse under single-game training. An empirical two-phase learning dynamics and scaling law. We observe an empirical early interface phase: model learns basic self-control and action primitives, which are shared and improved across worlds; and world phase then follows: further gains are obtained in trained and unseen related worlds. We fit an empirical scaling law to relate validation loss to data volume, mixture diversity and model size, achieving R2 = 0.982 and R2 = 0.995 on Minecraft and in-corpus loss, respectively. Three-level evaluation of model performance and generalization. We evaluate models in three-level setting covering in-corpus, related, and distant worlds. This anchors our models among game and general foundation models, providing the community reliable reference. Text:"Memory-Centric Continual Learning for AI Agents Autonomous agents powered by foundation models can reason, plan, and use tools, but their fixed weights and context limits hinder sustained learning from experience over long-term deployment. While external memory systems attempt to bridge this gap, they mostly act as passive retrieval caches, evaluated primarily by whether they can recall stored facts or past interactions. Whether memory can drive genuine continual learning remains an open question. In this paper, we introduce Memory-Centric Continual Learning (MCCL), arguing that memory should be judged by whether it improves future actions, not merely what it retrieves. MCCL has three main parts. First, three-axis taxonomy organizes memory by where state is stored (substrates), what form it takes (assets), and how it is managed (policies). This defines two-loop lifecycle that updates memory state in an inner loop while refining management policies in an outer loop based on task feedback. Second, an evaluation protocol measures retention under interference, adaptation to change, and task transfer to isolate genuine behavioral gains from simple context expansion. Third, memory scaling framework models how agent performance scales with experience, storage capacity, and compute budgets across non-parametric and parametric memory. Together, these components reframe agent memory from passive lookup to continuous learning, offering principled route toward self-improving autonomous agents. Non-parametric memory Parametric memory Figure 1: Memory scaling across two substrates. Theoretical illustrations: left, joint allocation of storage and reading resources sustains improvement beyond fixed-capacity limits; center, greater experience requires more parametric memory capacity; right, load-induced interference emerges at substrate-dependent thresholds. Curves are synthetic illustrations; see Section 4.3 and Figures 7 and 11. CONTENTS 1 The Landscape: Why Agents Need Continual "

The Landscape: Why Agents Need Continual Learning via MemoryAutonomous agents powered by foundation models have demonstrated strong capabilities in multistep reasoning, planning, and tool use across digital and physical domains [1, 111, 144]. However, current agents cannot reliably improve from experience over long deployments. Although modern agent harnesses can systematically track execution logs, timestamps, and environment states, they often treat accumulated history as raw text to be retrieved and inserted back into the prompt. Simply retrieving raw text does not guarantee behavioral improvement. As interaction trajectories grow, unmanaged context dilutes relevant signals, increases inference latency, and degrades model attention [120]. Conventional adaptation methods face clear trade-offs. Expanding context windows is bounded by session limits and suffers from attention degradation over long contexts. Meanwhile, continual fine-tuning requires substantial computation, risks catastrophic forgetting of general abilities, and lacks reliable mechanisms to roll back errors [39, 74]. Without mechanisms to distill, update, and transfer accumulated experience, deployed agents cannot retain what they learn. Consequently, they repeatedly commit the same errors, struggle with conflicting or outdated information, and fail to improve across sequential tasks [13, 47, 148]. Agent memory has emerged as primary approach to support persistence across sessions and personalization [19, 86, 87]. However, despite rapid progress, most existing methods focus on specific engineering implementations rather than systematic continual learning. Many approaches adopt an information retrieval view, treating memory simply as an external database or RetrievalAugmented Generation (RAG) cache designed to feed past tokens back into the prompt [61, 73]. Furthermore, existing classifications often blur distinct system choices, including where memory is physically stored, what form knowledge takes, and how memory operations are managed. Evaluation also remains limited. Many benchmarks primarily test whether an agent can recall or answer questions over static past history, rather than whether experience accumulated during deployment improves subsequent actions under interference and drift [29, 75, 125]. Moreover, as memory sto"

Text:"TraceForge: Distilling with Your Own Thinking Yan Wang1 1Independent Researcher yanwang.branden@gmail.com Using an LLM to generate training data for another is among the most general ways to train one, and it remains common practice. Some frontier teams have closed that route for their own models: what comes back is an answer, or short summary, without the detailed reasoning trace behind it. That is little ironic: they were themselves built on distilling human knowledge, and distilling an open-source model is still entirely routine; the restriction applies to their own outputs alone. Recent work recovers the hidden reasoning by replaying encrypted blocks across models [1]. It somehow works, but it breaks the providers terms of service, so it is not something product can be built on long-term. We built the alternative. We take the context and the teachers answer as input, and generate the reasoning trace ourselves, in the style of the students own model family. We call the result family-native trace. Training is two-stage, supervised and then adversarial: generator learns to regenerate the familys own reasoning traces from their context and answer, and critic learns how real reasoning trace looks. Applied to teachers answer, the generator writes one in the familys style. closed judge scores the family-native traces at 68% against 66% for the real ones, and at 82% once the critic picks the best of four. We test the idea on five benchmarks with everything else held fixed. On general tasks the five training corpora are about the same; on coding, the generated reasoning beats Fable 5s own thinking content. With the same 3,500 questions and the same teacher answers, HumanEval+ improves by 3.7 points and MBPP+ by 7.1 at the final checkpoint, and the MBPP+ lead holds at every checkpoint. Intuitively, we attribute this to the student and the family-native traces coming from the same model family: the student can absorb them without unlearning its own habits, so adaptation co"

Text:"October, 2026 Multimodal Embodied Agents: Unified Capability-centric Perspective from Computer-Use to Robot-Use Yiqi Lin Guoqiang Liang Kevin Yuchen Ma Kevin Qinghong Lin Mike Zheng Shou Show Lab, National University of Singapore ABSTRACT Multimodal agents (MMAs) sustain interaction through reasoning, memory, tools, and feedback, most visibly as computer-use agents, while robotic systems (RSs) couple sensing and actuation under physical dynamics. We define multimodal embodied agents (MMEAs) as goal-directed systems that couple multimodal task reasoning with physical action and revise decisions from the resulting feedback. This raises central question: what changes when multimodal agent moves from computer-use to robot-use? We introduce PAPAV, unified capability-centric framework of five recurring functions: Perceive the current state, Anticipate action effects, Plan feasible course, Act through an interface or body, and Verify the outcome. Defined by function rather than architecture, these capabilities let PAPAV identify what all three share and then compare MMEAs with MMAs and with RSs. Five physical constraints, from partial observability to unverifiable outcomes, leave fewer choices fixed in advance and less room to reverse errors. Across 64 benchmarks, explicit evaluation centers on Act; only 5 assess Anticipate and 3 assess Verify, leaving both largely hidden behind task success. These constraints also frame the open challenges we identify. PAPAV therefore provides common basis for designing reliable physical agents and measuring progress across all five capabilities rather than task success alone. Text:"October 15, 2026 DeLoop: Repeated Actions as Self-Supervision for Long-Horizon Agents Tianle Chen1,2, Pushkar Singh1,, Ketan Todi1, Biswajit Paria1, Qijia He1,3, Wenqi Wang1,2, Yuwen Tan1,2, Jike Zhong4, Sugato Basu1, Deepti Ghadiyaram2, Arjun Akula5, 1Google, 2Boston University, 3University of Washington, 4University of Southern California, 5Google DeepMind, Project lead Language-model agents that run long tasks often redo work they have already done: they re-issue tool call they made earlier and get back exactly the same observation, step that adds no new information. We identify this failure as loop. Loops appear natively in Qwen, Llama, and Gemini agents and become far more common once the agents history is compressed to fit its context window. To reduce these loops, we propose DeLoop, self-supervised training method. DeLoop builds on simple observation: the agents own rollout can provide the supervision needed to learn from its loops. We use looping action as negative example and the first later non-looping action in the same rollout as positive example. DeLoop learns from these pairs through direct preference optimization and does not require task rewards, gold answers, or LLM judges. On CodeGym under compression, DeLoop reduces the share of Qwen3-8B tasks containing loops from 57.3% to 6.1%. It also uses about one third as many steps per task, with similar task success. On Qwen3-32B, DeLoop reduces loops and steps while raising task success from 37.7% to 44.8%. Code and data will be publicly available. Keywords: LLM agents, long-horizon agents, agent memory, context compression, agent loops, repeated actions, preference optimization 1. Text:"ACE-Brain-0.6: Scaling Spatial Grounding for Physical Foundation Models ACE-Brain Team Please see Contributions and Author List for more author details.General-purpose multimodal models, including GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5, demonstrate broad capabilities in embodied manipulation. Our analysis indicates that this generality is accompanied by strong spatial perception, as Astra can determine where to interact with taskrelevant entities amid visual distractors. Because spatial perception connects high-level reasoning to the physical scene, we investigate scaling visual grounding to strengthen the perceptual foundation of general embodied operation. We develop unified data pipeline that combines robot manipulation recordings with complementary visual data from diverse sources. After automatically annotating the robot recordings, the pipeline converts the collected data into verifiable grounding supervision. Fine-tuning Qwen3.5-9B on approximately 1.5 million samples produced by this pipeline yields ACEBrain-0.6. The model outperforms GPT-6 Astra on four of seven public grounding benchmarks, with an overall average score of 75.41 compared with Astras 73.68. Under fixed Qwen3.5-9B fine-tuning setup, aggregate grounding performance improves steadily as successive data releases expand the training collection to cover broader range of grounding scenarios. These results show that scaling verifiable grounding supervision from diverse sources can strengthen the spatial perception of an open 9B model for general embodied operation. Grounding provides this connection by linking natural-language descriptions to their visual referents in an image [2, 3]. Reliable grounding requires precise localization of all image regions that satisfy description, with explicit recognition when no match exists [4]. In embodied environments, clutter and occlusion make this correspondence particularly difficult to establish [5]. grounding error can therefore misdirect an agents actions even when its high-level reasoning is sound. We therefore examine Astras spatial grounding capabilities in greater detail. Our analysis in Section 3 illustrates its ability to handle diverse grounding queries, while also revealing localization errors in tasks with precise spatial requirements. Text:"Think Harder Where It Matters: Item-Level Adaptive Recursion for Sequential Recommendation Jiakai Tang Gaoling School of Artificial Intelligence, Renmin University of China Beijing, China tangjiakai5704@ruc.edu.cn Zhanshuo Cao Gaoling School of Artificial Intelligence, Renmin University of China Beijing, China caozhanshuo@ruc.edu.cn Yuqi Zhou Gaoling School of Artificial Intelligence, Renmin University of China Beijing, China yuqizhou@ruc.edu.cn Sunhao Dai Gaoling School of Artificial Intelligence, Renmin University of China Beijing, China sunhaodai@ruc.edu.cn Jun Xu Gaoling School of Artificial Intelligence, Renmin University of China Beijing, China junxu@ruc.edu.cn Abstract Existing sequential recommenders usually allocate fixed computational budget to every item in users interaction history, regardless of the items contribution to the final prediction. We challenge this de facto design with two diagnostic findings. (1) Non-uniform computational need: items within the same sequence vary drastically in how much computation they require. Most converge after shallow processing while critical minority only stabilizes at full depth. (2) Suboptimality of fixed allocation: given oracle access to the target item, non-uniform depth assignment consistently outranks the fixed one, indicating that existing uniform computation both wastes capacity on already-converged items and sacrifices overall recommendation quality. These observations motivate shift from fixed-depth computation to item-level adaptive reasoning. To this end, we propose LoopRec. LoopRec recursively applies single weight-shared block and introduces lightweight gate at each recursion boundary that makes binary exit-or-continue decision for every item independently. Items whose representations have sufficiently matured exit early, while those requiring deeper reasoning continue to subsequent recursions. Santillana juan@jsantillana.com Independent research Abstract Perimeter triage faces latency/semantics trade-off: signature engines are fast and format-safe but blind to unseen attacks, while generative models are semantic but slow and produce unparseable output. In late 2026 class of commercial System One decision models (TypeSafe Jev; Microsoft-Decision-1) began selling typed decision primitivesbinary gates, closed-set choices, ordinal scoresas product APIs, with no academic treatment or independent evaluation. Text:"TOWARD PART-AWARE CHORAL TRANSCRIPTION WITH SINGING VOICE ASSIGNMENT Hanyu Meng1,4, Zhanhong He2,4, Zixun Guo3, Yaolong Ju4 1 The University of New South Wales, Sydney, Australia 2 The University of Western Australia, Perth, Australia 3 Center for Digital Music (C4DM), Queen Mary University of London, London, United Kingdom 4 Great Bay University, Guangdong, China ABSTRACT Single-instrument automatic music transcription (AMT) has advanced substantially, yet choral applications require soprano, alto, tenor, and bass (SATB) to be transcribed as separate parts. Recent note-level choral AMT instead produces single merged note track, limiting rehearsal, education, and score reconstruction. To address this limitation, we introduce Part-aware Choral Transcription (PawCT), to our knowledge the first end-to-end neural framework that identifies active SATB parts from choral audio and transcribes each into separate note-level track. PawCT combines part-specific onset, offset, and frame prediction with part-presence estimation, union-level supervision, and structured training targets using range prior (RP) based on SATB pitch ranges and its ordered-continuity (OC) extension, which adds within-part melodic continuity and cross-part pitch ordering. On YouChorale, PawCT-RP-OC achieves macro part-aware note F1 of 0.225 at 50-ms onset tolerance, outperforming an adapted choral baseline (0.165) by 36.4% relative and two-stage post-hoc assignment pipeline (0.175). Its part-agnostic variant, PagCT, achieves 50-ms onset F1 of 0.382, compared with 0.237 for the previous state-of-the-art choral AMT model. Crossdataset evaluations on CSD and Cantorıa further assess performance under dataset shift. These results demonstrate the benefit of jointly modeling note transcription and vocal-part assignment.1 Index Terms automatic music transcription, choral transcription, voice assignment, part-aware transcription 1. INTRODUCTION Automatic music transcription (AMT) converts audio into symbolic rep"

Abstract WildAWM synthesizes controllable computer-use environments and tasks to train general agents via interaction, improving out-of-distribution performance across web, CLI, and GUI benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agent learning is shifting from learning from human data toward learning through interaction with the world. However, scaling such learning directly in real-world computer systems is challenging and can introduce substantial security risks. We therefore propose Agent World Model in the Wild (WildAWM), a scalable pipeline for synthesizing controllable environments and tasks for training general computer-use agents (CUAs). Whereas existing approaches typically specialize in a single agent interaction interface, we construct executable environments that jointly support graphical user interfaces, model context protocol services, and command-line interfaces, allowing agents to flexibly combine visual interaction, tool use, and code execution. WildAWM is entirely powered by open-source models, demonstrating that high-quality computer-use environments can be generated without proprietary frontier models. Beyond environments, we address three recurring challenges in task synthesis: ensuring solvability, maintaining sufficient difficulty, and producing reliable verifiers, by introducing execution-based quality gates that combine heterogeneous agent rollouts, cross-judge consistency checks, and counterfactual probes of judgment robustness. The resulting pipeline synthesizes 341 long-horizon environment-task pairs that can require over 100 agent steps to solve and remain challenging even for frontier models, with GPT-6-Astra solving only 60% of them. We train two Qwen models of different sizes exclusively in these synthetic worlds. Both models achieve substantial gains across 8 out-of-distribution benchmarks spanning web, claw, and computer-use agents, demonstrating that learning in WildAWM's synthesized worlds generalizes broadly to unseen computer-use tasks. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/MSR-Orchard/WildAWM.

Среды поддерживают одновременно GUI, MCP-сервисы и CLI, поэтому агент может комбинировать визуальные действия, использование инструментов и выполнение кода. Авторы решают проблемы разрешимости, сложности задач и надёжности верификаторов с помощью quality gates на основе выполнения, кросс-судейских проверок и контрфактуальных тестов; весь пайплайн работает на open-source моделях. Модели Qwen, обученные исключительно в синтетических мирах, демонстрируют заметный прирост на 8 out-of-distribution бенчмарках, что говорит о хорошей обобщающей способности.", "emoji": "🌍", "title": "Учимся управлять компьютером в синтетических мирах"}

WildAWM is entirely powered by open-source models, demonstrating that high-quality computer-use environments can be generated without proprietary frontier models. Beyond environments, we address three recurring challenges in task synthesis: ensuring solvability, maintaining sufficient difficulty, and producing reliable verifiers, by introducing execution-based quality gates that combine heterogeneous agent rollouts, cross-judge consistency checks, and counterfactual probes of judgment robustness. The resulting pipeline synthesizes 341 long-horizon environment-task pairs that can require over 100 agent steps to solve and remain challenging even for frontier models, with GPT-6-Astra solving only 60% of them. We train two Qwen models of different sizes exclusively in these synthetic worlds. Both models achieve substantial gains across 8 out-of-distribution benchmarks spanning web, claw, and computer-use agents, demonstrating that learning in WildAWM's synthesized worlds generalizes broadly to unseen computer-use tasks. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/MSR-Orchard/WildAWM."

"Abstract WildAWM synthesizes controllable computer-use environments and tasks to train general agents via interaction, improving out-of-distribution performance across web, CLI, and GUI benchmarks. However, scaling such learning directly in real-world computer systems is challenging and can introduce substantial security risks. We therefore propose Agent World Model in the Wild (WildAWM), a scalable pipeline for synthesizing controllable environments and tasks for training general computer-use agents (CUAs). Whereas existing approaches typically specialize in a single agent interaction interface, we construct executable environments that jointly support graphical user interfaces, model context protocol services, and command-line interfaces, allowing agents to flexibly combine visual interaction, tool use, and code execution. WildAWM is entirely powered by open-source models, demonstrating that high-quality computer-use environments can be generated without proprietary frontier models. The resulting pipeline synthesizes 341 long-horizon environment-task pairs that can require over 100 agent steps to solve and remain challenging even for frontier models, with GPT-6-Astra solving only 60% of them. We train two Qwen models of different sizes exclusively in these synthetic worlds. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/MSR-Orchard/WildAWM."

[22.10.2026 05:49] Claude request. Model: claude-haiku-5-5. Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. 'emoji': emoji that will reflect the theme of an article somehow, only one emoji. 'title': a slogan of a main idea of the article in Russian. Return only JSON and nothing else.

Abstract AKV introduces cache engineering to manage LLM agent key-value caches without altering trajectories, improving efficiency and accuracy via drop and repos operations with a co-designed serving engine.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large Language Model (LLM) agents can handle increasingly complex tasks, navigating long trajectories of observations and actions. As these trajectories grow, key-value (KV) cache I/O and storage become the dominant cost. Trajectories can also exceed the LLM's maximum context length, requiring control over active cache growth. Prior work uses context engineering to reduce cache size by trimming or summarizing history. However, modifying the trajectory prefix invalidates subsequent cached states and triggers costly re-prefilling of retained context. Task accuracy can also suffer when too little context is retained to limit re-prefill costs. We propose cache engineering as a new scheme. It allows agents to directly control their active KV cache without modifying the trajectory or re-prefilling retained context. Specifically, agents can proactively use drop to exclude selected cache from future attention and repos to adjust retained keys' position encodings. We formalize these cache operations and identify history dependence as a serving challenge: identical token sequences can yield different cache states under different cache operation sequences, making prefix matching insufficient for correct cache reuse. Thus, we co-design the AKV serving engine. It provides a stateless interface compatible with existing serving APIs, while internally managing stateful cache reuse, eviction, and restoration. Experiments show that a tool-response rolling-drop cache policy improves average benchmark scores by 1.7 points and reduces average cost by 27.7%, compared with conventional threshold-triggered summarization. With AKV, it achieves up to 1.2x the output throughput and a 1.5x speedup in P95 request latency. Recursive self-improvement of cache policies further reduces cost by 7.1% over expert design. Our code is at https://github.com/thu-nics/AKV.

Агент может использовать операции drop, чтобы исключать часть кэша из attention, и repos, чтобы корректировать позиционные кодировки сохранённых ключей. Авторы также предлагают co-designed serving engine с stateless-интерфейсом, который внутренне управляет stateful-переиспользованием, вытеснением и восстановлением кэша. В экспериментах стратегия rolling-drop для ответов инструментов повышает средний балл на бенчмарках на 1.7 пункта, снижает стоимость на 27.7% и даёт до 1.2x пропускной способности и 1.5x ускорения P95-латентности.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Агент сам управляет своей памятью: KV-кэш без перестройки контекста"}

Abstract Future Chunk Prediction improves autoregressive video diffusion by enforcing future-chunk supervision, yielding more physically plausible video generation with reduced training cost.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Autoregressive video diffusion models generate videos chunk by chunk, yet producing plausible continuations does not guarantee that they capture how scenes evolve. Under teacher forcing, strong temporal redundancy may allow local visual continuity to serve as a shortcut for current-chunk denoising, reducing the pressure to model visual evolution. We introduce Future Chunk Prediction (FCP), an auxiliary objective jointly optimized with teacher-forced denoising that predicts temporally separated chunks without access to the intervening visual context. Drawing supervision directly from training videos, FCP requires no additional pretrained visual encoder and leaves autoregressive inference unchanged. Our method achieves superior physical plausibility over standard teacher-forced autoregressive training with a lower overall training compute budget, highlighting the effectiveness and efficiency of future-chunk supervision for modeling visual evolution. Intriguingly, our visualizations suggest that FCP fosters a more structured representation space, which may be valuable for building autoregressive world models.
[22.10.2026 05:50] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается Future Chunk Prediction (FCP) — вспомогательная функция потерь для авторегрессионных диффузионных моделей видео, которые генерируют ролики по чанкам. Проблема в том, что при teacher forcing модель может опираться на локальную визуальную непрерывность и не учиться моделировать эволюцию сцены. FCP обучает модель предсказывать удалённые по времени чанки без доступа к промежуточному контексту, используя supervision напрямую из обучающих видео и не требуя дополнительного pretrained visual encoder. Abstract Socratic Skill Learning improves reusable agent skills by interleaving expert guidance with student practice and targeted review, outperforming trajectory and contrastive distillation methods.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agent Skills improve LLM agents by making task knowledge reusable across sessions. Learning transferable Skills requires evidence about a procedure and its conditions of use, which a successful task attempt can leave untested. We introduce Socratic Skill Learning (SSL), which interleaves expert teaching with Student practice. An Educator guides investigation while the Student remains the sole environment operator and Skill author, able to question, test, and adapt advice. To test reuse beyond the learning task, we construct SkillTransferBench, with 35 task families and 408 executable cases, pairing public variation specifications with held-out evaluation in fresh solver sessions. On the main 22-family, 155-case cohort, Fixed-Window SSL reaches 0.801 utility, versus 0.723 without a Skill, 0.761 for adapted Trace2Skill (trajectory distillation), and 0.751 for adapted SKILL-KD (contrastive distillation). Across three model pairs on shared families, interleaved guidance outperforms expert authoring and post-hoc teaching. Process analysis links guidance to Student writing and package revision; a case study shows the Student testing and refining a proposed procedure for later reuse. As demand for new lessons falls, the cost of fixed-window review grows. Issue-Guided SSL tracks unresolved questions across Skill revisions to select and focus review, reaching 0.805 utility with 61.7% fewer Educator tokens and 16.9% fewer total acquisition tokens than Fixed-Window SSL on the main cohort.

Авторы создали бенчмарк SkillTransferBench из 35 семейств задач и 408 исполняемых кейсов для оценки переносимости навыков на новые сессии. SSL показывает лучшую полезность (0.801) по сравнению с траекторной дистилляцией Trace2Skill, контрастивной дистилляцией SKILL-KD и работой без навыков. Вариант Issue-Guided SSL отслеживает нерешённые вопросы между ревизиями навыка и сокращает расход токенов наставника примерно на 62% при сохранении качества.", "emoji": "🎓", "title": "Учитель задаёт вопросы, ученик сам проверяет навык, и знания переносятся на новые задачи"}

Abstract AgenticRoboRL improves robot planners via supervised fine-tuning and multi-turn reinforcement learning across diverse harnesses, significantly boosting success rates without external memory.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agentic robotics systems combine planners with robot harnesses to perform complex manipulation tasks. Recent work improves these systems through better harnesses and external memory, but relies on powerful closed-source models as planners and does not improve the planners' own capabilities. In contrast, coding agents have improved substantially by post-training the model itself. We therefore investigate whether post-training can also improve robot planners. However, applying it across robot harnesses requires accommodating different execution mechanisms, including VLA skill invocation and control-code generation. To this end, we introduce AgenticRoboRL, a framework for post-training robot planners across different harnesses. The training recipe combines supervised fine-tuning with multi-turn reinforcement learning. To accommodate different execution mechanisms, harness-specific adapters connect recorded planner interactions with task outcomes, enabling a common RL training pipeline while keeping execution components fixed. On LIBERO-PRO, AgenticRoboRL raises the success rate of a 9B-parameter planner from 18.1% to 71.0% without external memory, approaching the 72.1% achieved by Codex (GPT-5.5) with external memory. These results demonstrate post-training as an effective approach for improving robot planners. We will release the framework and trained planner checkpoints.

Метод сочетает supervised fine-tuning и многошаговое обучение с подкреплением (multi-turn RL), а специальные адаптеры связывают взаимодействия планировщика с результатами выполнения задач. Abstract Continued pre-training across data scale, diversity, and model size improves embodied learning in virtual worlds but reveals limits in distant-world generalization, motivating richer training beyond imitation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Scaling data, diversity and model capacity has improved embodied learning in physical environments, where diverse experiences remain grounded in shared physical laws. Whether these gains extend to virtual worlds with different visual styles, dynamics, controls, and objectives remains unclear. We investigate this question through a controlled study of continued pre-training (CPT) across three axes: data volume, diversity, and model size. Our 120 training configurations span four model sizes from 0.8B to 27B, six data budgets from 50 to 1,600 hours, and five mixtures ranging from Minecraft-only data to diverse multi-world corpora. We assess performance and generalization using three-level validation losses and gameplay benchmarks from in-corpus worlds to unseen worlds. Our results show: (1) more data improves in-corpus performance, diversity improves transfer to related worlds with limited gains in distant worlds, and model size improves data efficiency but generalizes worse to distant worlds under single-world training; (2) we observe an early interface phase with broad cross-world benefits and a later world phase with gains narrowed to in-corpus and related worlds, and fit an empirical scaling law relating validation loss to data volume, diversity and model size; and (3) our models are competitive with Minecraft specialists and frontier models in 3D worlds, and perform best in real-time settings. However, the limits of imitation and persistent gaps in distant worlds suggest that building generalist embodied agents requires richer training stages beyond continued pre-training.

Abstract Spectral-Spatial Interpretation assigns localized channel importance at each spatial position via conditional Shapley values, yielding full spectral-spatial attribution matrices that outperform existing methods on multispectral image tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Post-hoc image explanations usually show where a model looks using spatial heatmaps or indicate which channels matter using global channel scores. These separate views are limiting for multispectral data because channel relevance is often spatially localized and physically meaningful. We introduce Spectral-Spatial Interpretation (SSI), a full-grid attribution method over position-channel players (g,c). For each spatial position, SSI fixes the observed surrounding context, defines a conditional game over the channels at that position, and applies the Shapley value to this local game. The result is a full spectral-spatial attribution matrix rather than a single heatmap or a global channel ranking. SSI has a clear local interpretation. It satisfies Shapley fairness within each position, and aggregating its channel attributions exactly recovers the position-level contextual contrast obtained by replacing that position with the baseline. SSI also differs from context-averaged Owen-style hierarchical accounting, which uses the same position-channel grouping but averages over external spatial coalitions. On HLS Burn Scar, SSI outperforms an MC-Owen estimator across retained attribution metrics while using only about 6% of its evaluation budget. Across CUB-200, EuroSAT, and HLS Burn Scar under both per-channel mean and zero baselines, SSI achieves the best joint deletion and insertion AUC over dense, flat, and rank-1 baselines, yields the strongest spectral hit rates and channel deletion, and leads spatial localization on both annotated datasets. Code is available at https://github.com/AtheavenMa/Spectral-Spatial-Interpretation.
[22.10.2026 05:50] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет метод Spectral-Spatial Interpretation (SSI), который объясняет предсказания модели для мультиспектральных изображений, показывая важность каждого канала в каждой пространственной позиции. Для этого используется Shapley value, применённый к локальной условной игре над каналами при фиксированном окружающем контексте, что даёт полную spectral-spatial матрицу атрибуции. Метод сохраняет справедливость Shapley внутри каждой позиции и точно восстанавливает контрастность относительно baseline, отличаясь от Owen-подобных иерархических оценок. Эксперименты на CUB-200, EuroSAT и HLS Burn Scar показывают, что SSI превосходит аналоги по метрикам deletion/insertion, spectral hit rate и пространственной локализации при существенно меньшем бюджете вычислений.", "emoji": "🛰️", "title": "Где и какой спектральный канал важен — объясняем каждую точку изображения"}

Abstract Memory-Centric Continual Learning reframes agent memory as an active driver of behavioral improvement through a taxonomy of storage substrates, assets, and policies, a two-loop lifecycle, and scaling frameworks evaluated by retention, adaptation, and transfer.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Autonomous agents powered by foundation models can reason, plan, and use tools, but their fixed weights and context limits hinder sustained learning from experience over long-term deployment. While external memory systems attempt to bridge this gap, they mostly act as passive retrieval caches, evaluated primarily by whether they can recall stored facts or past interactions. Whether memory can drive genuine continual learning remains an open question. In this paper, we introduce Memory-Centric Continual Learning (MCCL), arguing that memory should be judged by whether it improves future actions, not merely what it retrieves. MCCL has three main parts. First, a three-axis taxonomy organizes memory by where state is stored (substrates), what form it takes (assets), and how it is managed (policies). This defines a two-loop lifecycle that updates memory state in an inner loop while refining management policies in an outer loop based on task feedback. Second, an evaluation protocol measures retention under interference, adaptation to change, and task transfer to isolate genuine behavioral gains from simple context expansion. Third, a memory scaling framework models how agent performance scales with experience, storage capacity, and compute budgets across non-parametric and parametric memory. Together, these components reframe agent memory from passive lookup to continuous learning, offering a principled route toward self-improving autonomous agents.

Abstract DONIC is a generalist bimanual tracker trained on diverse human-motion data that uses reinforcement learning with recycled demonstrations and per-hand rewards to achieve high success on unseen objects without fine-tuning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Despite recent progress in dexterous reference tracking, existing models focus primarily on single-hand manipulation and a limited range of interactions, leaving the bimanual dexterous behaviors common in real life unaddressed. The scarcity of contact-rich bimanual manipulation data and the increased complexity of coordinating contacts make developing a generalist bimanual tracker particularly challenging. Therefore, we present 1) DONIC, the first generalist bimanual tracker jointly trained across operation categories and human-motion sources, together with 2) BiHOI, a multi-view dataset of 1,575 sequences spanning 229 objects and emphasizing sustained bimanual interaction, where reconstructed interactions are retargeted into robot references. DONIC combines proximal policy optimization with behavioral cloning in a feedback loop that recycles verified successful executions into training demonstrations. An object-centered per-hand reward maintains object-motion feedback while supervising each hand according to its reference contact role. Clip-Centered Advantages remove reference-dependent advantage offsets while preserving within-trajectory differences. Together, these designs enable one single policy to learn coordinated manipulation across heterogeneous references. Without finetuning, a single checkpoint achieves 86.79% success on unseen trajectories of training objects and 57.32% on held-out objects, surpassing the state-of-the-art bimanual tracking baselines by 77.36 and 45.73 percentage points.

Модель использует обучение с подкреплением (PPO) в сочетании с поведенческим клонированием, а также возвращает успешные демонстрации обратно в обучающую выборку. Для каждой руки применяется объект-центрированная награда, а метод Clip-Centered Advantages устраняет смещения advantage, связанные с эталонной траекторией. Без дообучения одна модель достигает 86.79% успеха на траекториях знакомых объектов и 57.32% на новых, значительно превосходя существующие baseline-подходы.", "emoji": "🤲", "title": "Одна модель — две руки: универсальный бимануальный трекер без дообучения"}

Abstract The study proposes a two-stage supervised and adversarial method to synthesize reasoning traces matching a student model's style from teacher answers, improving coding benchmarks without relying on hidden outputs.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Using an LLM to generate training data for another is among the most general ways to train one, and it remains common practice. Some frontier teams have closed that route for their own models: what comes back is an answer, or a short summary, without the detailed reasoning trace behind it. That is a little ironic: they were themselves built on distilling human knowledge, and distilling an open-source model is still entirely routine; the restriction applies to their own outputs alone. Recent work recovers the hidden reasoning by replaying encrypted blocks across models. It somehow works, but it breaks the providers' terms of service, so it is not something a product can be built on long-term.   We built the alternative. We take the context and the teacher's answer as input, and generate the reasoning trace ourselves, in the style of the student's own model family. Training is two-stage, supervised and then adversarial: a generator learns to regenerate the family's own reasoning traces from their context and answer, and a critic learns how real a reasoning trace looks. Applied to a teacher's answer, the generator writes one in the family's style. An independent judge rates how likely traces are to be genuine on a scale from 0 to 1: ours average 0.68, real traces 0.66, and traces selected by the critic from four candidates 0.82. These are authenticity scores, not correctness rates.   We test the idea on five benchmarks with everything else held fixed. On general tasks the five training corpora are about the same; on coding, the generated reasoning beats Fable 5's own thinking content. With the same 5,000 questions and the same teacher answers, HumanEval+ improves by 3.7 points and MBPP+ by 7.1 at the final checkpoint, and the MBPP+ lead holds at every checkpoint. We suspect that reasoning written in the student's family style is easier for it to learn from.
[22.10.2026 05:51] Response: {"desc": "Статья предлагает двухэтапный метод (сначала supervised обучение, затем adversarial с критиком), который генерирует цепочки рассуждений в стиле модели-ученика по ответам учителя, без использования скрытых выходов закрытых моделей. Генератор учится восстанавливать рассуждения семейства моделей по контексту и ответу, а критик оценивает, насколько они похожи на настоящие. Такие синтетические трассы дают прирост на задачах программирования: HumanEval+ улучшается на 3.7, MBPP+ на 7.1 пункта при том же наборе данных. Авторы предполагают, что рассуждения, написанные в стиле собственного семейства модели, легче для неё усваиваются.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Учим модель рассуждать так, как рассуждает она сама"}

Abstract ALLOT is a training-free method that allocates block-sparse attention densities across layer-step pairs in video diffusion transformers to maximize output fidelity under a latency budget.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Full 3D attention is costly in video Diffusion Transformers. Block-sparse attention reduces this cost by computing only selected attention blocks. However, existing methods either overlook the potential to optimize sparsity allocation or rely on heuristic rules or local metrics to guide it. In this work, we observe that sparsifying attention in different layers and at different denoising steps affects final-video fidelity to substantially different degrees. Yet the identities of the most sensitive layer-step pairs remain nearly unchanged across inputs. Building on these findings, we propose Allocating Layer-step Latency by Output Tests (ALLOT), a training-free sparsity allocation method. Treating each layer-step pair as a cell, ALLOT estimates each cell's contribution to final-video error through (i) cell probing and (ii) trajectory calibration, then jointly optimizes cell densities under a measured-latency budget. It preserves existing selection strategies and kernels, replacing only the allocation component. At equal or shorter wall-clock time, ALLOT outperforms every compared allocation strategy with XAttention, with an 11.8 dB PSNR gain on HunyuanVideo. Extensive experiments across four additional published pipelines on Wan2.1, Wan2.2, and HunyuanVideo show that ALLOT outperforms or ties the default in all nine settings.
[22.10.2026 05:51] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен ALLOT — метод без дообучения (training-free) для распределения разреженности block-sparse attention между парами слой-шаг в video diffusion transformers. Авторы показывают, что чувствительность к разреживанию сильно различается по слоям и шагам денойзинга, при этом наиболее чувствительные пары почти не меняются от входа к входу. ALLOT оценивает вклад каждой ячейки в ошибку финального видео через probing и калибровку траектории, после чего оптимизирует плотности под заданный бюджет измеренной задержки. На HunyuanVideo метод даёт прирост 11.8 дБ PSNR относительно XAttention при том же или меньшем времени, а также не уступает базовой стратегии в девяти конфигурациях на Wan2.1, Wan2.2 и HunyuanVideo.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Умное распределение разреженности внимания ускоряет генерацию видео без потери качества"}

"Abstract ALLOT is a training-free method that allocates block-sparse attention densities across layer-step pairs in video diffusion transformers to maximize output fidelity under a latency budget.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Full 3D attention is costly in video Diffusion Transformers. Block-sparse attention reduces this cost by computing only selected attention blocks. In this work, we observe that sparsifying attention in different layers and at different denoising steps affects final-video fidelity to substantially different degrees. Abstract ALPS is a pretrained visual encoder that unifies physical-state prediction, language alignment, and pixel reconstruction to serve as a shared foundation for world models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small World models turn visual representations into predictions and actions. We argue that their visual foundation should combine physical-state information, native language alignment, and pixel reconstruction, so that a single representation can support prediction, instruction, and inspection. However, existing encoders typically specialize in only one or two of these capabilities; we therefore introduce ALPS, a pretrained visual state encoder that unifies all three in a single set of patch tokens. Using only still images and text, we pretrain a ViT-B/16 from scratch with language, reconstruction, and spatial correspondence objectives, and then refine it via cross-view self-distillation. In a unified evaluation across fifteen public encoders, ALPS achieves the lowest aggregate LIBERO state-probe error among ViT-B models (matching the 3.5x larger V-JEPA 2-L) while maintaining strong language alignment and accurate pixel reconstruction. Downstream, frozen ALPS tokens reach the highest mean policy success on LIBERO (85.5%) when paired with our native text tower, match or exceed leading encoders on world-model planning across PushT, Wall, and Granular, and allow predicted future tokens to be decoded directly into pixels for visual inspection. We hope this work helps establish visual pretraining as a shared foundation for understanding, prediction, and action in world models.

Модель обучается только на изображениях и тексте, используя языковые, реконструкционные и пространственные objectives, а затем дообучается методом cross-view self-distillation. На бенчмарках LIBERO ALPS показывает наименьшую ошибку state-probe среди моделей ViT-B и высокий процент успеха политик (85.5%) в связке с собственным текстовым encoder'ом. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract ALPS is a pretrained visual encoder that unifies physical-state prediction, language alignment, and pixel reconstruction to serve as a shared foundation for world models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small World models turn visual representations into predictions and actions. We argue that their visual foundation should combine physical-state information, native language alignment, and pixel reconstruction, so that a single representation can support prediction, instruction, and inspection. However, existing encoders typically specialize in only one or two of these capabilities; we therefore introduce ALPS, a pretrained visual state encoder that unifies all three in a single set of patch tokens. Using only still images and text, we pretrain a ViT-B/16 from scratch with language, reconstruction, and spatial correspondence objectives, and then refine it via cross-view self-distillation. In a unified evaluation across fifteen public encoders, ALPS achieves the lowest aggregate LIBERO state-probe error among ViT-B models (matching the 3.5x larger V-JEPA 2-L) while maintaining strong language alignment and accurate pixel reconstruction. Downstream, frozen ALPS tokens reach the highest mean policy success on LIBERO (85.5%) when paired with our native text tower, match or exceed leading encoders on world-model planning across PushT, Wall, and Granular, and allow predicted future tokens to be decoded directly into pixels for visual inspection. We hope this work helps establish visual pretraining as a shared foundation for understanding, prediction, and action in world models."

Abstract  Autonomous agents can increasingly propose ideas, modify code, and run experiments, creating an opportunity to accelerate scientific discovery using teams of collaborative AI researchers. To escape local minima in the discovery, we propose to encourage orthogonal exploration by constructing the team using agents with varying expertise. However, realizing this design requires more than diverse backgrounds. When experiments are costly, a team can test only a limited number of ideas, thereby creating the need to balance exploration and exploitation when selecting a subset of researchers to propose and execute new experiments. Furthermore, each result should shape what the team tries next. We therefore ask how AI researchers with different scientific backgrounds can build on one another's findings and sustain progress under a limited experimental budget. To address these challenges, we build a team of persistent AI researchers, each grounded in a different discipline, who keep private research histories, share experimental evidence, and receive research opportunities through adaptive allocation. We demonstrate the effectiveness of this design by deploying the team to search for linear attention architectures, where it outperforms GDN2 at 1.3B scale on language modeling, common-sense reasoning, and synthetic and real-world retrieval tasks. In controlled comparisons, removing any single organizational component from our design yields markedly weaker discoveries: without disciplinary backgrounds, the search plateaus early; without private histories, researchers make less progress despite identical shared evidence; and uniform selection falls short of adaptive allocation within the same budget. These results suggest that sustained autonomous discovery depends not only on stronger agents, but also on who explores, what they remember, and how research effort is allocated.

Система использует адаптивное распределение ограниченного бюджета экспериментов между агентами, балансируя exploration и exploitation. Abstract TextRIRO enables scene text editing using self-paired real data via image disruptions and a conditional diffusion model, improving realism and supporting downstream recognition.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Aiming to modify textual content while preserving the original style, Scene Text Editing (STE) has significant practical value in many applications. STE typically requires paired images before and after editing for training. However, recent approaches rely heavily on synthetic paired data and complex disentanglement strategies. They struggle to faithfully capture the diversity and complexity of natural scenes, often producing artifacts or hallucinations. In this work, we challenge the common practice and investigate whether STE can be driven by turning each real sample into its own paired supervision. Concretely, we explore multiple ways to disrupt the original image (e.g., Crop, Shuffle, Mask) to construct self-paired training data, and build a strong baseline (TextRIRO}) upon a conditional diffusion architecture. Our experiments show that combining character-column shuffling with image cropping (Shuffle&Crop) effectively destroys text semantics while preserving key style cues, enabling the model to learn editing behaviors directly from the real text image. With this concise yet real-oriented design, TextRIRO achieves promising performance in both accuracy and visual realism across real-world benchmarks, substantially mitigating the synthetic-to-real domain gap. Furthermore, we leverage TextRIRO to build TextRIRO-3M, a large-scale realistic dataset for long-text recognition by concatenating edited and original images. Experimental results show that training recognition models with TextRIRO-3M significantly improves recognition accuracy, demonstrating that TextRIRO provides highly useful training resources for downstream tasks. Overall, TextRIRO lifts STE to realistic, reliable, and widely applicable new levels. Code is available at https://github.com/YesianRohn/TextRIRO.
[22.10.2026 05:51] Response: {"desc": "Статья предлагает TextRIRO — метод редактирования текста на изображениях сцены, который обучается на self-paired данных: каждое реальное изображение искажается (crop, shuffle, mask) и служит собственной парой для supervised обучения. В основе лежит conditional diffusion model, а лучший вариант искажения — комбинация shuffle символьных колонок и crop, которая разрушает семантику текста, сохраняя стилевые признаки. Такой подход уменьшает domain gap между синтетическими и реальными данными и повышает реалистичность результатов. Авторы также создали датасет TextRIRO-3M для распознавания длинного текста, который улучшает качество downstream-моделей распознавания.", "emoji": "✍️", "title": "Каждое реальное фото — само себе учитель для редактирования текста"}

STE typically requires paired images before and after editing for training. However, recent approaches rely heavily on synthetic paired data and complex disentanglement strategies. They struggle to faithfully capture the diversity and complexity of natural scenes, often producing artifacts or hallucinations. In this work, we challenge the common practice and investigate whether STE can be driven by turning each real sample into its own paired supervision. Concretely, we explore multiple ways to disrupt the original image (e.g., Crop, Shuffle, Mask) to construct self-paired training data, and build a strong baseline (TextRIRO}) upon a conditional diffusion architecture. Our experiments show that combining character-column shuffling with image cropping (Shuffle&Crop) effectively destroys text semantics while preserving key style cues, enabling the model to learn editing behaviors directly from the real text image. With this concise yet real-oriented design, TextRIRO achieves promising performance in both accuracy and visual realism across real-world benchmarks, substantially mitigating the synthetic-to-real domain gap. Furthermore, we leverage TextRIRO to build TextRIRO-3M, a large-scale realistic dataset for long-text recognition by concatenating edited and original images. Experimental results show that training recognition models with TextRIRO-3M significantly improves recognition accuracy, demonstrating that TextRIRO provides highly useful training resources for downstream tasks. Overall, TextRIRO lifts STE to realistic, reliable, and widely applicable new levels. Code is available at https://github.com/YesianRohn/TextRIRO."

Abstract PAPAV defines five core capabilities for multimodal embodied agents and highlights gaps in evaluation and physical constraints when moving from digital to robotic settings.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multimodal agents (MMAs) sustain interaction through reasoning, memory, tools, and feedback, most visibly as computer-use agents, while robotic systems (RSs) couple sensing and actuation under physical dynamics. We define multimodal embodied agents (MMEAs) as goal-directed systems that couple multimodal task reasoning with physical action and revise decisions from the resulting feedback. This raises a central question: what changes when a multimodal agent moves from computer-use to robot-use? We introduce PAPAV, a unified capability-centric framework of five recurring functions: Perceive the current state, Anticipate action effects, Plan a feasible course, Act through an interface or body, and Verify the outcome. Defined by function rather than architecture, these capabilities let PAPAV identify what all three share and then compare MMEAs with MMAs and with RSs. Five physical constraints, from partial observability to unverifiable outcomes, leave fewer choices fixed in advance and less room to reverse errors. Across 64 benchmarks, explicit evaluation centers on Act; only 5 assess Anticipate and 3 assess Verify, leaving both largely hidden behind task success. These constraints also frame the open challenges we identify. PAPAV therefore provides a common basis for designing reliable physical agents and measuring progress across all five capabilities rather than task success alone.

Abstract StreamEval benchmarks interactive streaming video generation across five tasks, revealing that reliable request execution and sustained state consistency remain major challenges despite high visual quality.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small As streaming video generation advances, enabling real-time interaction with generated video is becoming increasingly important. We introduce StreamEval, a unified benchmark for interactive streaming video generation spanning five tasks: avatar dialogue, avatar performance, world generation, instruction editing, and reference editing. StreamEval comprises 300 scripted test cases lasting 15-300s, each specifying initial assets, incrementally arriving multimodal requests, and explicit requirements for state changes and content preservation. Performance is assessed along four evaluation dimensions: task execution, state consistency, visual fidelity, and audiovisual coordination, instantiated through ten task-specific metrics on a 0-100 scale. Evaluating 37 distinct models across 300 test cases, we find that high visual quality and identity retention do not necessarily coincide with reliable request execution or sustained state consistency. In particular, request execution remains challenging: the highest task-specific scores are 36.90 for Following among seven avatar-performance models, 25.93 for Following in text-conditioned world generation, and 15.00 for Edit Success among three reported reference-editing models. These findings identify reliable request execution under timing and preservation constraints as a central challenge for advancing omni-modal real-time interactive systems. The leaderboard is available at https://streameval.ai

Abstract GRSE improves cooperative language agents by comparing repeated executions to correctly assign behavioral credit to agent-skill pairs, outperforming prior methods on multi-hop reasoning and recovering from harmful skills.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Cooperative language-model agents can improve without weight updates by revising reusable skills, but only if a shared team reward is traced to the responsible agent and skill. Existing methods credit the skills present in each trajectory, yet presence is not responsibility: a harmful skill active in successful runs still earns positive credit. We present GRSE, Group-Relative Skill Evolution, which assigns behavioral credit by comparison: inspired by GRPO, it executes each question several times under the same skill libraries and contrasts the executions to credit agent--skill pairs, with structured diagnosis of failures. On HotpotQA and LoCoMo with two open-weight actors, GRSE outperforms MASkills, the closest prior method, on HotpotQA and every LoCoMo category except open-domain, with the largest gains on multi-hop questions (+6.7 and +3.2 F1). Its gains rest on better-grounded retrieval rather than answers given without evidence, and its learned skills transfer between the multi-hop tasks. GRSE is robust to corrupted libraries: starting from planted harmful skills, it credits every planted skill with the correct sign and recovers the score of a clean library (+6.3 points), whereas MASkills misattributes two and falls below its starting point.
[22.10.2026 05:52] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет GRSE (Group-Relative Skill Evolution) — метод улучшения кооперативных LLM-агентов без обновления весов, который revise-ит переиспользуемые skills. Ключевая идея: вместо того чтобы приписывать credit навыкам просто за присутствие в траектории, GRSE выполняет каждый вопрос несколько раз с одной библиотекой навыков и сравнивает запуски, как в GRPO, чтобы точно назначать credit парам агент–навык. Метод превосходит MASkills на HotpotQA и LoCoMo, особенно на multi-hop вопросах, и лучше обобщается благодаря более обоснованному retrieval. GRSE также устойчив к повреждённым библиотекам: он корректно определяет вредные навыки и восстанавливает качество чистой библиотеки.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Награждай не за присутствие навыка, а за его реальную пользу"}

Abstract DeLoop is a self-supervised preference optimization method that reduces repetitive tool-use loops in language-model agents by learning from their own rollouts, improving efficiency and success under context compression.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Language-model agents that run long tasks often redo work they have already done: they re-issue a tool call they made earlier and get back exactly the same observation, a step that adds no new information. We identify this failure as a loop. Loops appear natively in Qwen, Llama, and Gemini agents and become far more common once the agent's history is compressed to fit its context window. To reduce these loops, we propose DeLoop, a self-supervised training method. DeLoop builds on a simple observation: the agent's own rollout can provide the supervision needed to learn from its loops. We use a looping action as a negative example and the first later non-looping action in the same rollout as a positive example. DeLoop learns from these pairs through direct preference optimization and does not require task rewards, gold answers, or LLM judges. On CodeGym under compression, DeLoop reduces the share of Qwen3-8B tasks containing loops from 57.3% to 6.1%. It also uses about one third as many steps per task, with similar task success. On Qwen3-32B, DeLoop reduces loops and steps while raising task success from 37.7% to 44.8%.
[22.10.2026 05:52] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет DeLoop — метод самообучения с оптимизацией предпочтений (DPO), который снижает повторяющиеся вызовы инструментов у LLM-агентов. Повтор одного и того же действия, дающий идентичный результат, назван «петлёй» и становится особенно частым при сжатии истории агента. Обучение строится на собственных траекториях агента: зацикленное действие считается негативным примером, а первое последующее незацикленное действие — позитивным, без наград, эталонных ответов или LLM-судей. На CodeGym доля задач с петлями у Qwen3-8B снизилась с 57,3% до 6,1%, число шагов уменьшилось примерно втрое, а у Qwen3-32B успешность выросла с 37,7% до 44,8%.", "emoji": "🔁", "title": "Больше никаких пустых кругов: агент учится не повторяться"}

Abstract Harness Scaling improves agentic reinforcement learning by training across diverse execution configurations, boosting generalization to unseen harnesses.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agent harnesses have become a central component of modern agentic systems, shaping how models use tools, manage context, and coordinate long-horizon interaction. Realizing the benefits of these harnesses depends on how effectively the underlying model can make use of them, motivating reinforcement learning directly through the harness. However, existing approaches typically train through a single fixed harness, which can lead to specialization and limited transfer across harness configurations. To address this, we introduce Harness Scaling, a new scaling dimension for agentic RL that extends training from a single harness to diverse execution configurations. Concretely, we construct a factorized harness space, select a compact training subset with balanced factorial coverage, and organize cross-harness training to combine global coverage with controlled variation for the same task. Across multiple benchmarks, model scales, and RL algorithms, Harness Scaling consistently improves generalization to unseen white-box configurations. Moreover, models trained through structured white-box variation transfer effectively to unseen external black-box harnesses, outperforming models trained through a single complex black-box harness. These results establish harness diversity as a meaningful scaling dimension for agentic RL and highlight the importance of how this diversity is organized for cross-harness generalization.
[22.10.2026 05:52] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет Harness Scaling — новое направление масштабирования для агентного reinforcement learning, при котором обучение проходит не через один фиксированный harness, а через разнообразные конфигурации выполнения. Авторы строят факторизованное пространство harness-ов, отбирают компактное обучающее подмножество со сбалансированным покрытием и организуют кросс-harness обучение, сочетая глобальное покрытие с контролируемыми вариациями для одной задачи. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract Harness Scaling improves agentic reinforcement learning by training across diverse execution configurations, boosting generalization to unseen harnesses.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agent harnesses have become a central component of modern agentic systems, shaping how models use tools, manage context, and coordinate long-horizon interaction. Realizing the benefits of these harnesses depends on how effectively the underlying model can make use of them, motivating reinforcement learning directly through the harness. However, existing approaches typically train through a single fixed harness, which can lead to specialization and limited transfer across harness configurations. To address this, we introduce Harness Scaling, a new scaling dimension for agentic RL that extends training from a single harness to diverse execution configurations. Concretely, we construct a factorized harness space, select a compact training subset with balanced factorial coverage, and organize cross-harness training to combine global coverage with controlled variation for the same task. Across multiple benchmarks, model scales, and RL algorithms, Harness Scaling consistently improves generalization to unseen white-box configurations. Moreover, models trained through structured white-box variation transfer effectively to unseen external black-box harnesses, outperforming models trained through a single complex black-box harness. These results establish harness diversity as a meaningful scaling dimension for agentic RL and highlight the importance of how this diversity is organized for cross-harness generalization."

Abstract Agent4Sci evaluates frontier coding agents on scientific ML benchmarks, finding they improve results mainly through engineering tricks rather than domain insight, with targeted skills significantly boosting performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Frontier coding agents are increasingly framed as end-to-end automated researchers, and ML for science offers a demanding test of that promise: improving a scientific model often requires domain insight beyond routine ML engineering. We introduce Agent4Sci, a long-horizon testbed for measuring how far today's agents can improve the published best models on scientific ML benchmarks, spanning 20 benchmarks across 11 domains. Rather than ranking agents on a leaderboard, Agent4Sci asks what kinds of progress agents can make on these tasks under in-the-wild conditions, and where they fall short. Empirically, today's frontier agents advance the published best on every benchmark, with Claude Opus reaching 10.4% mean relative improvement and no run flagged for academic misconduct. These gains, however, come almost entirely from a bag of ML-engineering tricks rather than research-level insight. Trajectory analysis surfaces three recurring limitations in this setting: novelty miscalibration, resource under-utilization, and rapid collapse into local engineering sweeps. Lightweight plug-in skills for research ideation, experiment management, and scheduled checklists raise Claude Opus from 10.4% to 20.1% and Codex from 4.4% to 8.1%, while yielding more diverse, domain-relevant interventions. Agent4Sci gives the community a way to track and push frontier agents from ML engineering toward domain insight on real-world ML-for-science problems. Code and data are available on GitHub (https://github.com/maszhongming/agent4sci) and Hugging Face (https://huggingface.co/datasets/MingZhong/agent4sci).

Оказалось, что агенты стабильно улучшают результаты (Claude Opus — в среднем на 10,4%), но в основном за счёт инженерных приёмов, а не глубокого понимания предметной области. Анализ траекторий выявил три проблемы: неверная оценка новизны, недоиспользование ресурсов и быстрый переход к локальному перебору. Простые подключаемые навыки для генерации идей, управления экспериментами и чек-листов повышают результат Claude Opus до 20,1%, делая вмешательства разнообразнее и релевантнее домену.", "emoji": "🔬", "title": "От инженерных трюков к научному пониманию: агенты учатся думать как исследователи"}

Abstract SpatialBrain improves spatial reasoning by separating scene evidence from reasoning experience, using fused spatial memory, reusable skill procedures, and consolidated process records to guide tool use without altering the vision-language backbone.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Spatial reasoning requires more than access to geometric tools: a reasoner must decide when direct observation is sufficient and when to reconstruct, measure, or change reference frames. We present SpatialBrain, a framework that retains experience about these decisions while keeping its vision-language backbone fixed. The key distinction is between evidence about a scene and experience about how to reason with that evidence. 3DFusionMemory fuses current-scene observations into queryable spatial relations. A Spatial Skill Library provides reusable tool procedures, and Spatial Cognition associates task conditions with experience about when and how to use memory and skills. Completed trajectories are abstracted into process records and consolidated between task blocks, allowing later decisions to draw on earlier execution without transferring scene coordinates or reference answers. Across five spatial-reasoning benchmarks, SpatialBrain improves on direct answers from the same backbone on four and ties on the fifth. Component comparisons, experience-coverage curves, and traced resource decisions characterize how the framework accumulates and uses these resources.
[22.10.2026 05:52] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен фреймворк SpatialBrain, который улучшает пространственные рассуждения vision-language моделей (VLM), не изменяя их базовую архитектуру. Ключевая идея — разделение фактических данных о сцене и опыта того, как с ними рассуждать: 3DFusionMemory объединяет наблюдения в запрашиваемые пространственные отношения, а Spatial Skill Library хранит повторно используемые процедуры работы с инструментами. Модуль Spatial Cognition связывает условия задачи с опытом использования памяти и навыков, а завершённые траектории обобщаются в process records и консолидируются между блоками задач, без переноса координат сцены или эталонных ответов. На пяти бенчмарках SpatialBrain превосходит прямые ответы того же backbone на четырёх и сравнивается с ним на пятом.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Опыт решений, а не данные сцены: как обучить AI думать о пространстве"}

"Abstract SpatialBrain improves spatial reasoning by separating scene evidence from reasoning experience, using fused spatial memory, reusable skill procedures, and consolidated process records to guide tool use without altering the vision-language backbone.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Spatial reasoning requires more than access to geometric tools: a reasoner must decide when direct observation is sufficient and when to reconstruct, measure, or change reference frames. We present SpatialBrain, a framework that retains experience about these decisions while keeping its vision-language backbone fixed. The key distinction is between evidence about a scene and experience about how to reason with that evidence. 3DFusionMemory fuses current-scene observations into queryable spatial relations. A Spatial Skill Library provides reusable tool procedures, and Spatial Cognition associates task conditions with experience about when and how to use memory and skills. Completed trajectories are abstracted into process records and consolidated between task blocks, allowing later decisions to draw on earlier execution without transferring scene coordinates or reference answers. Across five spatial-reasoning benchmarks, SpatialBrain improves on direct answers from the same backbone on four and ties on the fifth. Component comparisons, experience-coverage curves, and traced resource decisions characterize how the framework accumulates and uses these resources."
