Abstract Scaling diverse verifiable visual grounding data improves spatial perception and embodied manipulation performance in open multimodal models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small General-purpose multimodal models, including GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5, demonstrate broad capabilities in embodied manipulation. Our analysis indicates that this generality is accompanied by strong spatial perception, as Astra can determine where to interact with task-relevant entities amid visual distractors. Because spatial perception connects high-level reasoning to the physical scene, we investigate scaling visual grounding to strengthen the perceptual foundation of general embodied operation. We develop a unified data pipeline that combines robot manipulation recordings with complementary visual data from diverse sources. After automatically annotating the robot recordings, the pipeline converts the collected data into verifiable grounding supervision. Fine-tuning Qwen3.5-9B on approximately 1.5 million samples produced by this pipeline yields ACE-Brain-0.6. The model outperforms GPT-6 Astra on four of seven public grounding benchmarks, with an overall average score of 75.41 compared with Astra's 73.68. Under a fixed Qwen3.5-9B fine-tuning setup, aggregate grounding performance improves steadily as successive data releases expand the training collection to cover a broader range of grounding scenarios. These results show that scaling verifiable grounding supervision from diverse sources can strengthen the spatial perception of an open 9B model for general embodied operation.

Чтобы усилить этот фундамент, они создают единый data pipeline, который объединяет записи работы роботов с разнородными визуальными данными и автоматически размечает их в верифицируемую supervision для visual grounding. Abstract PrismaForge uses a rubric-based pipeline to synthesize and filter high-quality multimodal code data, enabling open models that rival proprietary systems on visual coding tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multimodal code intelligence connects the symbolic world of programs with the visual world their execution creates. Interest in this capability is burgeoning, as models are increasingly asked to produce charts, webpages, animations, and semi-structured documents. Recently, proprietary models have pushed far ahead, producing interactive artifacts even from a text description or a screenshot alone. Yet their data and training remain closed, and open progress is held back by the quantity and quality of available data and by the pipelines and methods for producing it. Quality is the harder of the two, since a sample must be correct as a program, well built, and above all visually right once rendered. No existing signal covers all three. Execution often returns just a pass or fail, code similarity does not map to the visual output, and task-specific verifiers do not generalize. To push open progress further, we start from the data side with PrismaForge, a hierarchical data pipeline centered on a synthesized rubric that decomposes these factors into checkable criteria. The same rubric serves as both filter and reward: it curates the supervised corpus, then drives reinforcement learning on visual-coding tasks. Leveraging PrismaForge for data synthesis and filtering, we build PrismaCoder-2M, the most diverse open dataset for multimodal code intelligence and the largest to date. On the model side, we train PrismaCoder-9B and PrismaCoder-35B on this data and release both. They lead the open state of the art by a wide margin and rival leading proprietary systems. Our analyses further show that the proposed rubric-based pipeline improves multimodal coding performance across model backbones. We release our code and data at https://qiushisun.github.io/PrismaCoder-Home/.
[22.10.2026 05:52] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет PrismaForge — иерархический пайплайн для синтеза и фильтрации данных мультимодального code intelligence, где программный код связан с визуальным результатом его выполнения. Ключевая идея — синтезированный rubric (набор проверяемых критериев), который одновременно служит фильтром качества для supervised fine-tuning и функцией награды для reinforcement learning. На его основе собран датасет PrismaCoder-2M, крупнейший и самый разнообразный открытый датасет такого рода. Обученные на нём модели PrismaCoder-9B и PrismaCoder-35B обходят открытые аналоги и сопоставимы с ведущими закрытыми LLM на задачах визуального кодирования.", "emoji": "🎨", "title": "Рубрика как фильтр и награда: открытые модели учатся писать код, который хорошо выглядит"}

Abstract LoopRec enables adaptive per-item computation depth in sequential recommendation by using recursive shared blocks with early-exit gates, improving efficiency and accuracy.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Existing sequential recommenders usually allocate a fixed computational budget to every item in a user's interaction history, regardless of the item's contribution to the final prediction. We challenge this de facto design with two diagnostic findings. (1) Non-uniform computational need: items within the same sequence vary drastically in how much computation they require. Most converge after shallow processing while a critical minority only stabilizes at full depth. (2) Suboptimality of fixed allocation: given oracle access to the target item, a non-uniform depth assignment consistently outranks the fixed one, indicating that existing uniform computation both wastes capacity on already-converged items and sacrifices overall recommendation quality. These observations motivate a shift from fixed-depth computation to item-level adaptive reasoning. To this end, we propose LoopRec. LoopRec recursively applies a single weight-shared block and introduces a lightweight gate at each recursion boundary that makes a binary exit-or-continue decision for every item independently. Items whose representations have sufficiently matured exit early, while those requiring deeper reasoning continue to subsequent recursions. This design converts the fixed global depth into a learned, per-item property without auxiliary depth labels, and is readily adaptable to diverse backbone architectures. Extensive experiments on public benchmarks with four backbone families demonstrate that LoopRec consistently outperforms matched fixed-depth counterparts. Further analysis reveals that LoopRec's learned allocation exhibits interpretable patterns aligned with our post-hoc oracle analysis: lower-frequency items and more recent interactions naturally receive deeper computation. Our code is available at https://github.com/Elpmis117/LoopRec.

Авторы предлагают LoopRec: рекурсивно применяется один блок с общими весами, а на каждой границе рекурсии лёгкий gate принимает бинарное решение о выходе или продолжении для каждого элемента отдельно. Такой подход превращает фиксированную глубину в обучаемое свойство каждого элемента без дополнительных меток глубины и работает с разными backbone-архитектурами. Эксперименты показывают, что LoopRec стабильно превосходит аналоги с фиксированной глубиной, а редкие элементы и недавние взаимодействия получают более глубокие вычисления.", "emoji": "🔁", "title": "Не каждому элементу нужно одинаково много думать: адаптивная глубина вычислений для рекомендаций"}

Abstract  We present Qwen-Audio-3.1-ASR, a family of audio-language models that builds upon its predecesor Qwen-Audio-3.0-ASR with improved speech recognition and expanded capabilities in speaker-diarized transcription and general audio understanding. The family retains support for 30 languages and 16 Chinese dialects, together with instruction-controlled capabilities including historical-context conditioning, hierarchical hotword customization, and native transcription polishing. Speaker-diarized ASR produces structured outputs containing speaker labels, timestamps, and recognized text, maintaining consistent speaker labels across turns and the representation of overlapping speech. The general audio understanding capabilities cover audio-, speech-, and music-captioning, temporal grounding and sound event detection, as well as audio question answering and multiple-choice reasoning. These tasks broaden the application scope from recognizing spoken words to interpreting speakers, acoustic events, and their temporal relations. To accommodate different deployment requirements, the family includes variants for asynchronous file transcription, low-latency streaming, interactive voice input, and non-streaming recognition, with speaker-diarized transcription and general audio understanding available in certain variants. Evaluations span conventional ASR, multilingual and dialectal speech, speaker-diarized transcription, and general audio understanding, demonstrating the capabilities of the Qwen-Audio-3.1-ASR family across transcription and broader audio analysis tasks.

Abstract  Recent world action models provide visual foresight and future prediction for robot manipulation, but lack explicit mechanisms for compositional skill reuse. Agentic robotics combines code-based planning and reasoning with reusable skills, but often lacks predictive grounding in physical dynamics. We introduce AW-0, an agentic world action model framework that integrates code-based planning with joint video and action prediction. We jointly model code, future video, and robot actions as three coupled generative streams within a unified mixture-of-transformers architecture. Within this architecture a code expert generates parameterized skill routines and control logic while video and action experts generate future visual states and low-level motor commands. Code serves two complementary roles: it structures multi-step prediction and control within the world model and represents skills as reusable programs for subsequent agentic execution. We further introduce an automated curation pipeline that labels robot and human video trajectories with aligned programs and consolidates recurring routines into reusable skill libraries. Evaluations on RoboTwin 2.0 and physical bimanual manipulation tasks show improved task performance over VLA and WAM baselines, alongside gains in language following, semantic reasoning and robustness, demonstrating that agentic code enables connection between planning, prediction and execution within WAMs.

Abstract  People adapt how they talk to the person in front of them. However, chat models tend to respond in Standard English regardless of the dialect used by the user. A uniform answer can hide whether training treats these users alike, and output audits cannot tell as well. We trace how each post-training stage, from supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to preference optimization (DPO) and reinforcement learning, changes a model's tendency to match the user, and read what each update prefers from two content-matched replies, one matching the user and one not. Across 10 open model lineages, post-training standardizes replies to every informal cue, yet after adjusting for how likely each reply already was, the DPO update still favours matching British and Australian spelling over AAE and Singlish on 10 lineages, and models still produce AAE features when asked. We then introduce calibrated accommodation pairs (CAP), preference data that reward matching users' dialect density while preserving Standard English for Standard-English users. On a fully open model, CAP raises convergence on features it was not trained on without a detectable increase in dialect for Standard-English users or a cost in instruction following. In contrast, one-sided pairs reach a similar gain only by overshooting the user and imposing dialect. Our findings show that uniform replies can hide selective updates and audits should read the update as well as the output. Code: https://github.com/kouzhizhuo/cap-accommodation.

Abstract Extended reasoning in language models suppresses everyday scalar implicatures by initially considering enriched interpretations before canceling them, though early-stage answers can recover these inferences.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small When a speaker says ``some students passed'', listeners infer that not all of them did. Reasoning models now let users decide how long a model thinks before it answers, but we do not know what extra thinking does to such everyday inferences. We study this directly: we keep a model's weights fixed, change only how much it thinks, and record which reading it chooses. A second tool cuts the model's own reasoning at successive points and asks for an answer, showing where the reading changes. Thinking makes models read logical words as logic: it cancels the implicatures of some (not all) and or (not both) in most tested open reasoning models and in GPT-5, while context-driven inferences such as indirect answers improve. The trace shows why: models first entertain the enriched reading and cancel it later. Answering from that early reading, an exploratory inference-time remedy, recovers these implicatures without losing literal accuracy. Evaluations of reasoning models should measure how they interpret, not only how accurate they are.
[22.10.2026 05:53] Response: {"desc": "Авторы изучают, как длина рассуждений (extended reasoning) у языковых моделей влияет на скалярные импликатуры, например вывод «не все» из фразы «some students passed». Веса модели остаются неизменными, меняется только объём размышлений, а с помощью частичного обрыва цепочки рассуждений находят момент, когда меняется интерпретация. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract Extended reasoning in language models suppresses everyday scalar implicatures by initially considering enriched interpretations before canceling them, though early-stage answers can recover these inferences.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small When a speaker says ``some students passed'', listeners infer that not all of them did. Reasoning models now let users decide how long a model thinks before it answers, but we do not know what extra thinking does to such everyday inferences. We study this directly: we keep a model's weights fixed, change only how much it thinks, and record which reading it chooses. A second tool cuts the model's own reasoning at successive points and asks for an answer, showing where the reading changes. Thinking makes models read logical words as logic: it cancels the implicatures of some (not all) and or (not both) in most tested open reasoning models and in GPT-5, while context-driven inferences such as indirect answers improve. The trace shows why: models first entertain the enriched reading and cancel it later. Answering from that early reading, an exploratory inference-time remedy, recovers these implicatures without losing literal accuracy. Evaluations of reasoning models should measure how they interpret, not only how accurate they are."

Abstract  Most contemporary artificial intelligence (AI) systems are released with frozen parameters and improve through external updates, whereas an ideal system would keep improving from its own experience, a capacity we call recursive self-improvement (RSI). Yet studying RSI requires an environment that is continuously changing, objectively verifiable, and naturally grounded, posing a fundamental challenge for agent evolution. Here we introduce DeepEvolve, a sustainable evolution agent system grounded in future event prediction, in which agents continuously interact with a dynamically evolving environment and improve by internalizing and externalizing the experience gained through these interactions. A living environment synthesizes verifiable tasks, and a recursive-evolution engine turns the reasoning trajectory behind each prediction back into capability: decoupling search from prediction adapts parameter-level evolution to the contamination-prone live environment, while distilling reusable skills lets the agent evolve from the limited live trajectories such an environment affords. On the external FutureX leaderboard, which evaluates predictions against real-world outcomes and is updated weekly, our self-improved forecaster ranked first across all systems over three consecutive weeks, achieving up to a 195% improvement in rating. On our synthesized evaluation, it ranked among the top three across four base LLMs, outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 10.7 Bradley--Terry points. More broadly, these results suggest that a system built for self-improvement is best understood as an agent that co-evolves with a genuinely living, open-ended environment, pointing to a shift in AI from training static models on fixed data toward cultivating agents that grow stronger from their own experience, with such an open world as the indispensable basis of that growth.

Агент решает верифицируемые задачи прогнозирования будущих событий, а рассуждения, лежащие в основе каждого прогноза, превращаются в обновление параметров и в переиспользуемые навыки. Abstract  As LLMs expand their language coverage, evaluating how well they understand and represent diverse cultures becomes increasingly important. However, many benchmarks remain English-centric, with limited cultural coverage and a focus on factual knowledge over its application in real-world cultural contexts. We propose CulturalRiddles, a benchmark of original, culturally grounded riddles created entirely by humans through a participatory community effort, spanning 62 cultures (45 countries, 51 languages). These riddles are deeply rooted in their respective cultures and require abductive and compositional reasoning, alongside detailed factual and social knowledge, language skills, and an understanding of culturally specific associations. We benchmark 28 LLM configurations using both automatic and large-scale human evaluation. Even frontier models achieve at most 22% accuracy in some communities. We publicly release all artifacts alongside an open contribution process to expand the benchmark's linguistic and cultural coverage.

"Abstract  As LLMs expand their language coverage, evaluating how well they understand and represent diverse cultures becomes increasingly important. However, many benchmarks remain English-centric, with limited cultural coverage and a focus on factual knowledge over its application in real-world cultural contexts. We propose CulturalRiddles, a benchmark of original, culturally grounded riddles created entirely by humans through a participatory community effort, spanning 62 cultures (45 countries, 51 languages). These riddles are deeply rooted in their respective cultures and require abductive and compositional reasoning, alongside detailed factual and social knowledge, language skills, and an understanding of culturally specific associations. We benchmark 28 LLM configurations using both automatic and large-scale human evaluation. Even frontier models achieve at most 22% accuracy in some communities. We publicly release all artifacts alongside an open contribution process to expand the benchmark's linguistic and cultural coverage."

Abstract RubatoCodec uses adaptive event-based coding with fixed utterance budgets to improve low-rate speech reconstruction and streaming text-to-speech generation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Neural speech codecs provide discrete tokens for speech generation. Lower frame rates shorten token sequences, but each token must then carry more linguistic and acoustic information. Variable-frame-rate codecs address this trade-off by adapting token allocation to speech content, but threshold-based allocation lets content determine the token count, so the sequence length a speech generator must produce varies across utterances and datasets at a fixed setting. We present RubatoCodec, a variable-frame-rate speech codec built on X-Codec-2 with an exact utterance-level event budget and streaming decoding. Its Adaptive Firing mechanism uses changes in codec features and waveform spectra to divide speech into variable-duration events without a learned boundary predictor. Discrete codes and an explicit duration represent each event, allowing a speech generator to predict and decode one event at a time. Controlled experiments show that adaptive allocation outperforms fixed-rate coding in reconstruction fidelity and intelligibility at low event rates. A model trained on large-scale multilingual speech achieves competitive reconstruction quality across English, Japanese, and Mandarin, with the best intelligibility preservation among the compared codecs on the evaluated English and Japanese benchmarks at comparable bitrates. In a matched text-to-speech comparison, the adaptive representation yields lower WER and higher UTMOS and UTMOSv2 with comparable speaker similarity. The speech generator also learns the codec's utterance-level event budget without an explicit generation-time rate constraint. These results demonstrate that budgeted adaptive speech representations support both high-quality reconstruction and streaming text-to-speech generation. Code and weights are available at https://github.com/third-intelligence/RubatoCodec and https://huggingface.co/third-intelligence/RubatoCodec.
[22.10.2026 05:53] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен RubatoCodec — нейросетевой речевой кодек с переменной частотой кадров и точным бюджетом событий на высказывание. Механизм Adaptive Firing делит речь на события переменной длительности по изменениям признаков кодека и спектра сигнала, без обучаемого предсказателя границ. Каждое событие описывается дискретными кодами и явной длительностью, что позволяет языковой модели генерировать и декодировать речь по одному событию в потоковом режиме. Эксперименты показывают лучшее качество восстановления и разборчивость при низкой частоте событий, а также меньший WER и более высокие UTMOS/UTMOSv2 в задаче text-to-speech.", "emoji": "🎙️", "title": "Адаптивные события вместо фиксированных кадров: бюджетная речь для потокового синтеза"}

Abstract VX-One is a small multi-task neural network that outputs calibrated typed decision primitives for perimeter triage, evaluated in a two-phase cascade against signature engines and baseline classifiers across multiple intrusion detection benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Perimeter triage faces a latency/semantics trade-off: signature engines are fast and format-safe but blind to unseen attacks, while generative models are semantic but slow and produce unparseable output. In late 2026 a class of commercial System One decision models (TypeSafe Jev; Microsoft-Decision-1) began selling typed decision primitives - binary gates, closed-set choices, ordinal scores - as product APIs, with no academic treatment. We present VX-One: a small multi-head network (100K-10M parameters) whose outputs are these primitives - a calibrated gate, a choice over a closed candidate set that always includes an explicit unknown option, and an ordinal score, trained multi-task, temperature-calibrated per head (~0.7-1.7 ms p50 on CPU). We evaluate it on UNSW-NB15, CIC-IDS2017, EMBER2024 against calibrated XGBoost, Random-Forest, MLP, Kitsune, and measure a two-phase cascade (Suricata with 52,674 Emerging-Threats rules -> VX-One) over five days of CIC-IDS2017. Findings: (i) VX-One wins detection (AUROC/FPR@95%TPR) on UNSW-NB15 and under temporal drift on CIC-IDS2017 (0.711 vs 0.637), but XGBoost wins multi-class attribution on all three benchmarks; (ii) leave-one-family-out gate detects never-seen families on 9/9 UNSW folds yet drops below chance on CIC recon family, and abstention threshold transfers poorly across benchmarks; (iii) on payload-less reconstructions signature engines resolve ~0.1% of flows - gray zone ~99.9% of traffic, recovered by Phase 2 (combined recall >=0.978); (iv) calibration collapses under temporal drift (ECE 0.04->0.40) for every method while ranking partially survives. We provide a measured account of where typed calibrated decisions help perimeter triage, and where they fail.

Abstract PawCT is an end-to-end neural framework that jointly transcribes choral audio into separate SATB note tracks using part-specific predictions, range priors, and ordered-continuity constraints.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Single-instrument automatic music transcription (AMT) has advanced substantially, yet choral applications require soprano, alto, tenor, and bass (SATB) to be transcribed as separate parts. Recent note-level choral AMT instead produces a single merged note track, limiting rehearsal, education, and score reconstruction. To address this limitation, we introduce Part-aware Choral Transcription (PawCT), to our knowledge the first end-to-end neural framework that identifies active SATB parts from choral audio and transcribes each into a separate note-level track. PawCT combines part-specific onset, offset, and frame prediction with part-presence estimation, union-level supervision, and structured training targets using a range prior (RP) based on SATB pitch ranges and its ordered-continuity (OC) extension, which adds within-part melodic continuity and cross-part pitch ordering. On YouChorale, PawCT-RP-OC achieves a macro part-aware note F1 of 0.225 at a 50-ms onset tolerance, outperforming an adapted choral baseline (0.165) by 36.4% relative and a two-stage post-hoc assignment pipeline (0.175). Its part-agnostic variant, PagCT, achieves a 50-ms onset F1 of 0.382, compared with 0.237 for the previous state-of-the-art choral AMT model. Cross-dataset evaluations on CSD and Cantoria further assess performance under dataset shift. These results demonstrate the benefit of jointly modeling note transcription and vocal-part assignment. Code and demos are available at https://hanyu-meng.github.io/Paw_Choral_AMT_Demo/.

Модель совместно предсказывает onset, offset и frame для каждой партии, оценивает присутствие партии и использует range prior на основе диапазонов SATB и ограничения ordered-continuity для мелодической непрерывности и порядка высот между партиями. На датасете YouChorale PawCT-RP-OC превосходит адаптированный хоровой baseline и двухэтапный пост-обработочный пайплайн по метрике part-aware note F1 при 50-мс допуске onset.
